## 2026-11-07 · M4

### Prior art on generating process models from text: block reference stands (AKS-131)

- **Decision:** spec 2 "Block reference" stands unchanged for all seven M4 blocks (`queue`, `batch`, `unbatch`, `store`, `resource_pool`, `schedule`, `failure`), per `docs/research/spec-2-prior-art.md`. Each block matches a construct that at least two of the sources read need, and none of them shows a setting, port or behaviour that is wrong or missing for the M4 benchmarks and acceptance rows. The block behaviour defaults (AKS-76) are not decided: prior art supports stating a preempt-resume rule for `failure` (breakdown with repair was the mechanism generated code got wrong most often, through the remaining-service bookkeeping), and neither supports nor questions the other defaults. The note's notes for the MCP tools (model files only, errors as data, behaviour defaults in `get_schema`, small edits, no per-component seeds, a `failure` or `resource_pool` assistant case) are recorded for M7, not built.
- **Reason:** guide rules 3 and 5; spec 2 "Research before building" (M4). Eight studies that generate process models from text (four writing free code, four generating a structure that existing code runs) and three process-mining and business-process-simulation sources were read, with the simmer block library for comparison. The free-code studies fail on mechanisms (routing between nodes, breakdown with resume, calendars, buffers, lead times) that the M4 blocks fix once as declarative settings. The block names and settings in spec 2 agree with `PROCESS_TYPES` in `src/lcsim/model/catalogue.py` and with the adapter's capabilities (the note's "Tested").
- **Alternatives considered:** adding an order-up-to level or a periodic review to `store`, a source schedule for time-varying arrivals, or partial availability to `schedule`: none is needed by an M4 benchmark or acceptance row, and each is a new policy rather than a defect; left for the owner below.
- **Awaiting owner:** (1) spec 2's summary table says a source makes arrivals "by distribution or from a schedule", but the binding "Block reference" gives a source no schedule and a `schedule` targets only a server or pool; under the spec's own rule the binding text wins, so Phase 1 has no time-varying arrivals (simplest reading). Chen et al. (arXiv:2601.06543) treat piecewise arrivals as a common mechanism. Either the summary is corrected or a later version adds a source schedule. (2) Constructs the sources need with no Phase 1 block: reneging, assembly of different parts, order-up-to or periodic `store` policies, partial or probabilistic availability and multitasking (classes and priority are already deferred to Phase 2).
- **Spec sections updated:** none. The note adds no spec change, so spec 2 "Changes in this version" gets no "Since version 0.6" line from it.
- **Dependencies added:** none.

### `queue`, `batch` and `unbatch` blocks and their benchmarks (AKS-105)

- **Decision:**
  - **Queue.** `Queue` in `src/lcsim/engines/process/blocks.py` holds up to `capacity` units in a deque. It does not `send`: it offers its next unit to the next block and, when refused, puts one step in that block's `blocked` line; the step takes the unit the discipline picks at the moment space frees (`fifo` the oldest, `lifo` the latest to arrive by then), so `lifo` works with blocking. An item passing straight through waits 0 s and is never counted as waiting, even for no time: `queue_length` rises only while the queue's offer waits in the next block's `blocked` line and falls with each departure, so after every instant it equals the units held. A unit counts against `capacity` from arrival until it moves. With `on_full` `block` (default) a full queue refuses, so a server feeding it keeps its unit busy and a source feeding it discards (`<source>.discarded`, the "Source when blocked" default); with `discard` it always accepts and an item arriving when it is full leaves the model at once, counted in `<queue>.discarded`, out of work in progress, with no wait. Space freed in the queue moves the units blocked upstream at the same instant, through the replication's `Transfers`, so a line of 2,000 full queues frees without nesting. `shortest_queue` counts every unit a queue holds (spec 2 speaks of a server's waiting items and "0 for a block without a queue"; a queue's whole content is its queue). Proposed defaults, owner to confirm.
  - **Statistics of a queue.** `<queue>.wait.mean`: arrival at the queue to the moment the unit moves to the next block, over units moving on after warm-up (at or after it, as for every observation); a pass-through counts 0 s; a discarded item is not counted. `<queue>.queue_length.mean` and `.max` follow the server's rules (time-weighted over the period; the maximum includes the value held at the end of warm-up). `<queue>.discarded` and `<source>.discarded` are discards at or after warm-up over the period. Each confirms its "proposed; confirmed at M4" row of spec 2 with the precise reading written there. Owner to confirm.
  - **Time series.** Each queue adds `<queue>.queue_length`, in component order, since spec 2 records "every time-persistent statistic" and `queue_length.mean` is one; its time average is that metric. A batch has none: its only statistic, `wait.mean`, is a mean over items, not time-persistent. Owner to confirm.
  - **Batch.** `Batch` collects arriving units in a list; at `size` it forms a batch: an `Item` whose `contents` are the units in arrival order and whose `created` is the first unit's ("A batch's cycle time is its first item's", read as the first unit to arrive at the batch). With `timeout`, each batch's first unit starts a SimPy timeout; a counter of formed batches makes a timeout due after its batch filled do nothing, so it never releases the next batch early. A formed batch is handed on with `send`: if the next block refuses it, it waits in that block's `blocked` line while the next batch collects, so a batch always accepts and a source feeding a batch never discards (matching validation, where a batch is not a block that can refuse). `<batch>.wait.mean` runs from each unit's arrival to the moment its batch moves on to the next block, which is later than forming when that block refuses it ("Arrival at the batch to its release", read as release from the block, like a queue's wait), over the units of batches moving on at or after warm-up, whatever their arrival time. Arrival times are taken when the batch forms, because the blocks downstream overwrite an item's `arrived`. Owner to confirm.
  - **Unbatch.** `Unbatch` sends on, at once and in order, the `contents` of what it receives, one level of batching (a batch of batches gives batches, as validation's "undoing one level" says), and a single item unchanged ("an `unbatch` splits whatever it receives"). It always accepts; units the next block refuses wait in that block's `blocked` line in order. No metrics, no time series (the catalogue lists none).
  - **Batches elsewhere.** A batch is one unit everywhere: one place in a queue's `capacity` or a server's queue, one service. A sink receiving a batch (no unbatch before it) counts it once in `count` and `throughput`, with one cycle time from the batch's creation time; that is where "a batch's cycle time is its first item's" takes effect, since items after an unbatch keep their own creation times. Work in progress counts items (`Item.count()`), so a batch reaching a sink, or discarded by a queue, removes every item it holds. Owner to confirm.
  - **Translate.** `queue`, `batch` and `unbatch` are in `BLOCK_TYPES`, so `translate` accepts them with the catalogue's defaults filled in; `store`, `resource_pool`, `schedule`, `failure` and a server's `pool` still raise `NotImplementedError` naming M4 (AKS-106, AKS-107).
  - **Benchmarks.** `benchmarks/queue_loss` (seed 1006) and `benchmarks/batch_unbatch` (seed 1007), both 100 replications of 200 h with 10 h of warm-up, laid out as the M2 folders, rows `three_se` and `M4`, values the exact closed forms in SI units: `exit.throughput` 37/5250 per second and `arrivals.discarded` 9/7000 per second (M/M/1/3, P₃ = 27/175, Sztrik section 5.4, table 4, as `docs/research/spec-2-benchmark-sources.md` cites); `batch.wait.mean` 120 s and `exit.throughput` 1/60 per second. Every arrival and service time is exponential, as for the M2 files; spec 2 gave "30/h" and means. The seeds were chosen before any run (the next after the M2 seeds 1001 to 1005, in the ticket's order) and were not changed. `CURRENT_MILESTONE` in `tests/benchmarks/conftest.py` is `M4`, so the M4 cases run; `test_every_m2_block_appears_in_an_m2_benchmark` is unchanged (AKS-107 widens it).
  - **Validation.** `src/lcsim/model/validation.py` already propagated W201 rates through `batch` (divides by `size`) and `unbatch` (multiplies by the size of what it receives) and stopped them at loss points, with static tests in `tests/model/test_validation_w.py`; nothing there changed. `tests/engines/process/test_flow_rates.py` now runs deterministic models and checks that the blocks pass exactly the rates `flow_rates` computes, that a source feeding a batch is not a loss point, and that at a loss point less gets through than is offered.
- **Reason:** spec 2 "Block reference", "Rules that apply across blocks" (Blocking, Branch rules), the "Batch timeout" and "Source when blocked" proposed defaults (AKS-76, unanswered, so built to them; no benchmark showed one wrong), "Statistic definitions" and "Benchmark definitions"; spec 1 "Validation" (W201) and "Tables for the `process` domain"; guide rule 5. Spec 2 did not say whether a pass-through counts as waiting, when a queue's wait ends, what `lifo` picks under blocking, which item is a batch's first, when a batch's wait ends if it is refused, whether a batch or an unbatch can refuse, what a sink does with a batch, how work in progress counts one, whether a queue has a time series, or what `shortest_queue` counts at a queue; each reading above is the simplest one consistent with the rest of the spec.
- **Alternatives considered:**
  - Counting every item that enters a queue as waiting, even for no time: `queue_length.max` would be 1 or more in any queue that ever passed an item, unlike a server whose free unit takes an item without it waiting.
  - A queue that sends each item as it arrives, like a server's finished item: the move would be fixed in refusal order, so `lifo` could not pick the latest item when space frees.
  - Ending a batch's wait when it forms, even if refused: the time a refused batch spends at the block would count nowhere, while a queue's wait covers it.
  - A batch that refuses while a formed batch waits: its source would discard items, and validation would have to treat a source feeding a batch as a loss point, which spec 1 does not list.
  - A sink counting the items inside a batch, each with its own cycle time: "a batch's cycle time is its first item's" would then never apply anywhere.
  - A batch that keeps its own creation time (its forming): contradicts that rule.
- **Spec sections updated:** spec 2 the warning line, "Rules that apply across blocks" (Blocking, Branch rules, new "Queue" and "Batch and unbatch"), "Batch timeout", the paragraph after the defaults, "Statistic definitions" (the `queue_length.mean`, `<queue>.wait.mean`, `<batch>.wait.mean` and discards rows; time series), "Benchmark definitions" (how the M4 files read the table), the acceptance table and "Changes in this version" › "Since version 0.6"; spec 1 "Tables for the `process` domain" (`timeseries` metrics) and "Changes in this version" › "Since version 0.6".
- **Dependencies added:** none.
- **Tested:**
  - `tests/engines/process/test_queue.py` (19 tests), computed by hand event by event with constant times: a full queue making its source discard (4 of 10; waits, queue length mean and max, cycle times, work in progress), the same with `discard` counted at the queue, `lifo`, `fifo` as the default, pass-through never counted waiting (also for three items arriving together), a full queue blocking the server upstream (utilizations 220/400 and 390/400, waits, queue length), the warm-up boundary (an arrival at exactly the warm-up counts; the maximum takes the value held then), discards before warm-up not counted, NaN wait when no item leaves after warm-up, the `<queue>.queue_length` series, `accepts()` in both modes, items moving in order when space frees, space in the queue freeing the block upstream, 2,000 full queues freeing without recursion, unknown words refused.
  - `tests/engines/process/test_batch.py` (13): forming at `size` with contents and creation time, a batch reaching a sink counted once with its first item's cycle time and work in progress counting items, items after an unbatch keeping their own cycle times, a timeout releasing pairs, a filled batch's timeout not releasing the next batch, size 1 with a timeout, a partial batch at the end not counted, the warm-up boundary (at 0, 50 and 300 s), a refused batch waiting while the next collects (waits 60 to 220 s, no discards), no time series.
  - `tests/engines/process/test_unbatch.py` (6): registration, a batch split in order at one instant, a single item passing through, nested batches split one level, refused items waiting at the unbatch in order behind a server with `queue_capacity` 0, always accepting.
  - `tests/engines/process/test_flow_rates.py` (6): the W201 rates against the running blocks (batch, unbatch, a server after a batch, two loss points, a source feeding a batch).
  - `tests/engines/process/test_translate.py`: the three types translate with defaults; `store` is refused naming M4.
  - Mutation checks outside the suite: removing the timeout's batch counter, counting every arrival in `queue_length`, reading a batch's arrival times after the move, dropping the `min` rule for departures, and counting a batch as one item in work in progress each make at least one test fail.
  - Benchmarks at their fixed seeds: `queue_loss` `exit.throughput` 25.341 per hour (−1.13 SE from 25.371), `arrivals.discarded` 4.595 per hour (−1.54 SE from 4.629); `batch_unbatch` `batch.wait.mean` 120.03 s (+0.21 SE from 120), `exit.throughput` 60.04 per hour (+0.81 SE from 60). Coverage run, by hand, `pytest -m benchmark_coverage -k "queue_loss or batch_unbatch" -s`: `queue_loss` 20 of 20 seeds from 1006, `batch_unbatch` 20 of 20 from 1007 (77 s).
  - `bash scripts/check.sh` exits 0 on Python 3.13 (1,742 passed, line coverage of `src/lcsim` 100%; 1,745 after the review fix below, still 100%); `pytest -m benchmark` passes 7 cases (the five M2 cases and the two above), with the `queue_loss` estimates unchanged by the fix.
- **Review of PR #27 (Codex):** one finding, reproduced and fixed with tests that failed before the fix. With `batch(size=3) → unbatch → queue(capacity=1, on_full=discard) → sink`, the unbatch hands its three items on in one instant, and each offer is a separate transfer step. The second item reached the queue before the queue's own offer of the first had run, so the queue counted as full and discarded it, although the sink would have taken every item (sink count 3 of 9, `line.discarded` 6 per 600 s). Before a queue now counts as full, in `receive` and in `accepts`, it moves on the units the next block takes at that instant (`_drain`, then `_advance`, which moves units while the next block accepts and otherwise waits in its `blocked` line). A guard stops a queue that feeds itself from draining itself from within its own `_advance`. Every earlier hand-computed test is unchanged. New tests: `test_items_arriving_together_are_not_discarded_while_they_can_move_on` (9 items reach the sink, 0 discarded, `queue_length.max` 0), `test_a_queue_whose_items_can_move_on_is_not_full`, and `test_a_full_queue_feeding_itself_holds_its_item` (which hit `RecursionError` before the guard). Spec 2 "Queue" now says so.
- **Second review of PR #27 (Codex):** one finding, reproduced and fixed. A queue that feeds itself with room for the item (blocking with `capacity` 2, or discarding) circled it forever inside one `_advance` loop: each `_take` called the queue's own `receive`, so `waiting` never emptied, and the loop never went back to `Transfers.run` to ask `should_stop`; the new test hung until it was killed. `_advance` now moves only the units held when it starts. A unit arriving meanwhile gets an offer of its own among the transfers, so the loop runs one transfer step per pass and ends with `Stopped` like the zero-time branch loop of AKS-99. Test: `test_a_queue_feeding_itself_can_still_be_stopped` (both modes). As with that branch loop, such a model never finishes on its own; flagging it at validation would need a new issue code, which is left to the owner. `bash scripts/check.sh` exits 0 (1,747 passed, coverage 100%); `pytest -m benchmark` passes 7 cases, and the `queue_loss` estimates are unchanged.
- **Third review of PR #27 (Codex), and the redesign it led to:** a burst from an unbatch into a long chain of queues of one raised `RecursionError` (reproduced with 400 items and 400 queues in both modes, once the unbatch receives inside a transfer step, as it does in a run). This was the third finding on the same mechanism: the first fix made `accepts()` and `receive()` drain the queue synchronously, and a drain asked the next queue's `accepts()`, which drained in turn, one level per queue. The root cause is fixed rather than patched. `accepts()` now only reads the queue, and every onward move is a `Transfers` step (`_offer`, `_pull`, `_take`, each queuing the queue's `space_freed`), so no call nests one level per block. A blocking queue that is full only because its own offer is still due refuses the arriving item; the item moves in at that same instant when the offer frees space, in refusal order (spec 2 "Blocking"), so nothing is lost or delayed. A source never meets such a queue, because the transfers of every earlier event have finished before a source's event runs. A discarding queue in that state holds the item in `_arrivals` and queues a decision step (`_decide`) after the offer: each held item enters while there is room, is discarded only if the queue is still full with its offer refused, and otherwise waits for the next offer. Later arrivals join `_arrivals` behind it, so the decisions keep arrival order. `waiting_items()` counts the held items too. The `_drain`, `_advance` and `_advancing` guard of the earlier fixes are gone; the bound of the second fix (an offer moves only the units held when it starts) stays in `_offer`. All three earlier tests are kept: the burst is not discarded, a queue feeding itself holds its item, and a queue feeding itself can still be stopped. `test_a_queue_whose_items_can_move_on_is_not_full`, which tested the synchronous drain, is replaced by `test_a_blocking_queue_takes_a_burst_in_order_at_the_same_instant` (`accepts()` is False within the step; both items reach the next block at 0 s in order; `queue_length.max` 0). New: `test_a_burst_through_a_long_chain_of_queues_does_not_recurse` (both modes; `RecursionError` before the redesign) and `test_a_burst_is_discarded_once_the_queue_is_full_with_its_offer_refused`, computed by hand (5 of 9 discarded behind a busy server). Every earlier hand-computed test is unchanged. At their fixed seeds the five M2 benchmarks and both M4 benchmarks give the same estimates as before, to every digit printed, including `queue_loss`. Spec 2 "Queue" now states the rule rather than the mechanism. `bash scripts/check.sh` exits 0 (1,750 passed, coverage 100%); `pytest -m benchmark` passes 7 cases.

### `store` block and the deterministic store benchmark (AKS-106)

- **Decision:**
  - **Store.** `Store` in `src/lcsim/engines/process/blocks.py` keeps the stock on hand (a whole number, `initial` at time 0), the units on order and a first-in-first-out line of items waiting for stock. An item arriving while stock is on hand takes one unit and is sent on at that instant with `send`; one arriving at zero stock waits. A store always accepts, so a source feeding one never discards. An item that has its unit but is refused by the next block keeps the unit (it is no longer on hand) and waits in that block's `blocked` line, in refusal order, like an item at a delay or a branch ("Blocking": the item stays where it is). Proposed default, owner to confirm.
  - **Reorder.** The store reviews its stock once at time 0 (`start`) and after every withdrawal, nowhere else: an item finding no stock withdraws nothing and changes neither stock nor orders. "Falls to `reorder_point`" is read as "stock on hand plus on order is at or below `reorder_point`"; each such review places exactly one order of `order_quantity`, so several orders can be outstanding, and a position far below the reorder point orders once per withdrawal. Waiting items are not subtracted from the position, since the block reference writes "stock on hand plus on order". The time-0 review orders when `initial` ≤ `reorder_point`, so an empty store starts its first order without waiting for a withdrawal that cannot happen. With unit withdrawals the position passes through every integer on the way down, so "at or below" and "exactly at" agree whenever the position starts above the reorder point, as in the benchmark. Owner to confirm.
  - **Deliveries and the lead-time stream.** Each order draws its `lead_time` from the store's own stream (`<seed>:<replication>:<store id>`) when it is placed, and its delivery is a SimPy timeout that long after. A delivery runs as one `Transfers` step: it adds the units, then the waiting items take them in arrival order, each withdrawal reviewing the stock; their moves onward are later transfer steps, never nested calls, and `accepts()` only returns True. A delivery due at or after the end of the run never arrives (spec 2 "Simultaneous events").
  - **Stock-outs.** An item is a stock-out when it finds the stock at zero, counted when it arrives (at or after warm-up), whether or not it gets a unit later; an item arriving before warm-up and released after it is not counted. `<store>.stockouts` is a count of items, like `<sink>.count` and `<source>.created`, not a rate: spec 2 says "items", and the catalogue already gave it the kind `number` (AKS-90).
  - **Other statistics.** `<store>.stock.mean` is the time-weighted stock on hand over the observed period (integrated from warm-up, the value held at warm-up included). `<store>.orders` counts orders when placed, at or after warm-up, divided by the period: a rate in items per second, as the catalogue's kind `rate` says. Each confirms its "proposed; confirmed at M4" row with the readings written there.
  - **Queue length and time series.** A store has no `queue_length` metric (the catalogue lists none and adding one would add metric names); `waiting_items()`, which `shortest_queue` reads, counts the items waiting for stock, not those refused downstream (as a server counts its queue, not its units). Its one time series is `<store>.stock`, the stock on hand, whose time average is `stock.mean`; stock-outs and orders are counts, not time-persistent, so they have none, although spec 2's summary says each statistic comes with a time series (the binding text wins).
  - **Translate.** `store` is in `BLOCK_TYPES`; `resource_pool`, `schedule`, `failure` and a server's `pool` still raise `NotImplementedError` naming M4 (AKS-107). The refusal test now uses a `resource_pool`.
  - **Benchmark.** `benchmarks/store_deterministic` (seed 1008, the next after the AKS-105 seeds, chosen before any run; nothing draws from it, since every time is constant): source `arrivals` every 6 min from 0 s, store `store` (100, 30, 100, constant 123 min), sink `exit`; 2 replications of 1,000 h with 100 h of warm-up, as the table states (spec 2: the stochastic defaults apply "unless stated"; `test_each_model_loads_with_zero_issues_and_the_spec_2_settings` now takes the stated settings for this case). Three `exact` rows, milestone M4: `store.stock.mean` 60.0, `store.orders` 1/36,000 per second, `store.stockouts` 0.
  - **The known answers re-derived (guide rule 7, cycle arithmetic only).** In minutes, demands at 6k from 0. The position first falls to 30 after 70 withdrawals, at 414; that order arrives at 537 with 30 − 20 = 10 on hand (20 demands, 420 to 534), making 110. Thereafter every cycle is 100 demands, 600 min: an order at 414 + 600n (the 80th withdrawal after a delivery, on hand 30, nothing on order) and a delivery at 537 + 600n, 3 min after a demand. Over a cycle from a delivery, the stock is 110 for 3 min, each of 109 down to 11 for 6 min, and 10 for 3 min: (330 + 6 × 5,940 + 30)/600 = 36,000/600 = 60.0. The observed period [6,000, 60,000) min is 54,000 min, exactly 90 cycles, so the average is 60.0 whatever the phase; orders at 414 + 600n for n = 10 to 99 fall in it, 90 over 900 h = 0.100 per hour; the stock never goes below 10, so 0 stock-outs. Spec 2's 60.0, 0.100/h and 0 agree. Under the "at or below" reading the 123 min lead time does what spec 2 says it was chosen for: deliveries fall at 537 + 600n, never a multiple of 6, so never at a demand; and since the position starts above 30 the two readings of "falls to" give the same orders here.
  - **Validation.** `src/lcsim/model/validation.py` already stopped the W201 rate at a store (spec 1 "Validation"), with a static test in `tests/model/test_validation_w.py`; nothing there changed. `tests/engines/process/test_flow_rates.py` adds a running model showing why the skip is right: the source's rate would load the server below the store at 1.5 and raise W201, but the store's orders let one item through per 120 s lead time and the server never queues.
- **Reason:** spec 2 "Block reference" (`store`), "Rules that apply across blocks" (Blocking, Branch rules), "Statistic definitions" and "Benchmark definitions"; spec 1 "Random streams", "Validation" (W201) and "Tables for the `process` domain"; the council's proposed default that store demand takes one unit and waits at zero, counted as a stock-out (AKS-76, unanswered, so built to it; the benchmark does not contradict it); guide rule 5. Spec 2 did not say what "falls to" means, whether several orders can be outstanding, whether the stock is reviewed at time 0, what an item refused downstream does, when a stock-out is counted, whether stock-outs are a count or a rate, what `shortest_queue` counts at a store, or which time series a store has; each reading above is the simplest consistent with the rest of the spec.
- **Alternatives considered:**
  - "Falls to" as "exactly at" the reorder point: a store starting at or below it (an empty store with reorder point 0 included) would never order, and an empty store would hold every item for ever.
  - Ordering enough multiples of `order_quantity` to lift the position above the reorder point (the textbook (s, nQ) policy): it is a different policy from "an order of `order_quantity` is placed"; left for the owner with the order-up-to policies the prior-art note lists.
  - Subtracting waiting items from the position (inventory position net of backorders): the standard textbook definition, but not what spec 2 writes; with it a long stock-out could place many orders at once. Left for the owner.
  - No review at time 0: an empty store would never order, since an item finding no stock withdraws nothing.
  - Counting a stock-out when the waiting item gets its unit: items still waiting at the end would not count, though they found the stock at zero.
  - `<store>.stockouts` as a rate, like discards: spec 2 says "items", and the catalogue's kind is `number`.
  - A store that refuses while items wait for stock: spec 2 says the item waits, and its source would then discard.
  - A `<store>.queue_length` metric or series: not in spec 2's statistics or the catalogue; adding one is a new metric name for the owner.
- **Spec sections updated:** spec 2 "Rules that apply across blocks" (Blocking, Branch rules, new "Store"), the paragraph after the defaults, "Statistic definitions" (the three store rows; time series), "Benchmark definitions" (how the store file reads the table), the acceptance table and "Changes in this version" › "Since version 0.6"; spec 1 "Random streams", "Tables for the `process` domain" (`timeseries` metrics) and "Changes in this version" › "Since version 0.6".
- **Dependencies added:** none.
- **Tested:**
  - `tests/engines/process/test_store.py` (21 tests), computed by hand event by event with constant times: depletion, reorder, delivery and stock-outs released in order (4 orders, 6 stock-outs, stock mean 90/600, cycle times); the warm-up boundary at 30, 120 and 150 s (an order or a stock-out at exactly the warm-up counts; one before does not, even if released after); an empty store ordering at time 0; the time-0 review with no arrivals; three orders outstanding; a position below the reorder point ordering at every withdrawal; first-in-first-out release by a delivery; `accepts()` and `waiting_items()`; an item refused downstream keeping its unit (stock mean, utilization 1, no discards); a burst from an unbatch; a burst of 400 through 400 stores and a delivery of 400 into 400 stores without recursion; a store feeding itself with a 0 s lead time stopped by `should_stop`, between events and inside one; lead times equal to the first draws of the store's stream; the store's draws leaving the source's arrivals unchanged; the `<store>.stock` series; levels and metrics.
  - `tests/engines/process/test_flow_rates.py`: the store's W201 skip against a running model. `tests/engines/process/test_translate.py`: a store translates; `resource_pool` is refused naming M4.
  - Mutation checks outside the suite: "exactly at" for "at or below", no review at time 0, stock-outs counted at release, last-in-first-out release, and ordering several multiples at once each make at least one test fail.
  - Benchmark `store_deterministic` at seed 1008: `store.stock.mean` 60.0, `store.orders` 2.777777777777778e-05 per second, `store.stockouts` 0.0, every replication equal. The seven earlier cases give the same estimates as on the base commit (59979dc) to every digit (compared with a script printing every estimate, before and after).

### `resource_pool`, `schedule` and `failure` blocks and their benchmarks (AKS-107)

- **Decision:**
  - **Capacity that changes over time (the M2 engine gap).** SimPy's `Resource.capacity` is read-only, so the adapter keeps the counts itself, as the M2 "Engine gap" entry chose between its two workarounds: a server or pool (`Units` in `src/lcsim/engines/process/blocks.py`) has its nominal `capacity`, a `scheduled` capacity and a number of `failed` units, and the available capacity is min(scheduled, nominal) − failed, at least 0. An item takes a unit only while fewer units hold items than are available. Nothing stops an item when the capacity falls; a rise runs as a `Transfers` step that starts waiting items and then moves the items blocked upstream, so a shift start, a repair or a pool release never calls into a downstream block's move logic synchronously (the AKS-105 lesson). This is event logic and bookkeeping, not a numerical method (guide rule 7). A server or pool with neither a schedule nor a failure computes every statistic exactly as before (`utilization` is busy units over capacity), so no earlier result changes; with either, `utilization` is the integral of the busy units, counted at each instant up to the available capacity, over the integral of the available capacity, and NaN when that integral is 0 (no capacity over the whole observed period), like a mean over no items. Owner to confirm.
  - **Pool.** A server with `pool` takes one of its own units, then asks the pool (`ResourcePool.take`): the unit is granted at once when nobody waits and one is available, otherwise the request joins one line shared by every server of that pool, first come first served by the moment of asking. A released pool unit goes to the head of the line in a `Transfers` step. The item's wait and service begin when it holds both units ("service, and therefore the end of the item's wait, begins only when both are held"). While it holds a server unit and waits for the pool it is waiting, not in service: it counts in `<server>.queue_length` and in the server's waiting items for `shortest_queue` (so the M/M/1 pool case has the M/M/1 queue length, 2.25), and its unit is not busy in `<server>.utilization` (0.375 in that case). `queue_capacity` limits only the items waiting for a server unit. A finished item refused downstream keeps both units until it moves, and both are released together then, as "Blocking" says a refused item keeps its capacity ("both are released after service" read as after the item has left the unit). A pool has no ports, so it is never a branch's route; a `schedule` may target a pool (spec 2's catalogue allows it) and changes its capacity the same way. A pooled server's `utilization`, `scheduled_fraction` and `availability` are over its own units; a schedule on its pool changes none of them. `<pool>.utilization` is the granted pool units (a unit held by a refused finished item counts) over the pool's units, by the same rule as a server's. Owner to confirm.
  - **Schedule.** The capacity at time t is the sum of the `capacity` of every shift with `start` ≤ t mod `period` < `end`, 0 where none is listed: overlapping shifts add up (the simplest reading of a list of shifts; no new error). It never exceeds the target's own `capacity`: a larger value is taken as the nominal capacity, since the target has no more units, so `scheduled_fraction` is at most 1 (no new error either). The capacity at time 0 is set when the blocks are bound, before any event, so an item arriving at 0 already meets it; each later change is a SimPy timeout of the schedule's process, so a change and an arrival at one instant run in scheduling order (in `test_the_default_period_is_24_hours_and_the_shift_list_repeats` the shift start at 24 h, scheduled at 1 h, runs before the arrival at 24 h, scheduled at 12 h). A rise starts waiting items in the order the discipline gives; a fall stops nothing: a unit holding an item finishes it and, if refused, holds it until it moves ("finishes its current item, then stops"). The time series of `utilization` under a schedule or failure is the busy units, at most the available ones, over the available ones, and 0 while none is available; its time average is no longer the summary metric (a ratio of two integrals has no such series). `<server>.scheduled_fraction` is the integral over the observed period of min(scheduled, nominal) over nominal times the period. Owner to confirm.
  - **Failure.** One `failure` block is one alternating process: it draws a `time_to_failure` from its own stream at time 0, fails one unit when it has passed, draws a `time_to_repair`, repairs the unit when that has passed, draws the next `time_to_failure`, and so on. So one unit at a time fails ("one unit at a time" in the AKS-107 description; "a failure takes one unit"), the time to failure runs on calendar time from each repair, busy, idle and off shift alike, and `time_to_repair` runs from the moment of failure. The failed unit counts against the scheduled capacity (spec 2 defines the available capacity as "scheduled capacity minus failed units"), so a unit failing off shift still leaves the next shift one unit short until its repair. Which unit fails, in order: an available unit holding no item; otherwise the unit of the item in service that started service last, which is suspended (its end cancelled, its remaining time kept) and resumes on the repaired unit at the repair with that remaining time, even if its shift has ended (it is the unit's current item); otherwise a unit holding a finished item refused downstream, which still moves on when space frees (its service is done); otherwise a unit whose item waits for a pool unit, whose service then begins only at the repair (it may get and hold the pool unit meanwhile). A suspended item is neither busy (its unit is failed) nor waiting (its wait ended when its service began), so it adds nothing to `queue_length` or to `wait.mean`. A unit freed while failed stays out of service until the repair. `<server>.availability` is the time-weighted fraction of the observed period in which fewer units than `capacity` are failed, 1 without a failure; with one process per block, a server of capacity 2 or more always has availability 1. Owner to confirm.
  - **Streams.** A failure block draws from `<seed>:<replication>:<failure id>` only, interleaving its two distributions in event order; a schedule and a pool draw nothing; a server's service time is still drawn when its service starts, and a resumed item draws nothing. So adding a schedule, a pool or a failure leaves every other component's random numbers unchanged (tested).
  - **Translate.** `resource_pool`, `schedule` and `failure` are in `BLOCK_TYPES`, which now holds every type of `PROCESS_TYPES`, so `translate` refuses no block and no setting: its `NotImplementedError` path and the "not yet" settings map are gone. An `empirical` with `data` is still E162 until M6. Blocks naming other blocks find them in a new `bind` step after the connections, before any block starts.
  - **Benchmarks.** `benchmarks/resource_pool` (seed 1009) holds both cases of spec 2's row in one model, as two lines sharing nothing (the harness runs one experiment per case and asserts one scenario; each component draws from its own stream, so the lines are independent): rows `server_a.wait.mean` 270 s, `server_a.queue_length.mean` 2.25, `server_a.utilization` 0.375, `pool_a.utilization` 0.75 (M/M/1), `server_b.wait.mean` 810/7 s and `pool_b.utilization` 0.75 (M/M/2), all `three_se`. The rows beyond the two waits check the readings above. `benchmarks/failure` (seed 1010), the M/M/1 file plus failure `breakdown`: `server.availability` 0.8, `three_se`, the only row, since the other results of this heavily loaded server (0.9375 of its available capacity) converge slowly from an empty start. `benchmarks/schedule_deterministic` (seed 1011, nothing draws): `server.wait.mean` 28,800 s, `server.utilization` 0.375 and `server.scheduled_fraction` 1/3, `exact`, with 2 replications of 204 h and 12 h of warm-up in `STATED_SETTINGS`. Every arrival and service time in the two stochastic cases is exponential, as for the earlier files. The seeds were chosen before any run, the next three after 1008 in the ticket's order, and were not changed.
  - **The deterministic schedule answers re-derived (guide rule 7, event by event).** In hours, arrivals at 1 + 8k, so three a day at 24d + 1, 24d + 9 and 24d + 17; the shift is [24d, 24d + 8). Day 0: the item of 1 h serves 1 to 2; those of 9 and 17 h wait. Each later day the shift opens with two items waiting: the one of 24(d − 1) + 9 serves 24d to 24d + 1 (wait 15 h), and at 24d + 1 the arrival of 24d + 1 (scheduled at 24d − 7) runs before that service's end (scheduled at 24d), so it joins the queue behind the item of 24(d − 1) + 17, which serves 24d + 1 to 24d + 2 (wait 8 h); the new item serves 24d + 2 to 24d + 3 (wait 1 h). The other order gives the same waits, as the queue is first in first out. No service crosses 24d + 8. The observed period [12, 204) holds days 1 to 8 whole on shift and none of day 0's shift: 24 starts, waits 8 × (15 + 8 + 1)/24 = 8 h; busy 8 × 3 = 24 h over 8 × 8 = 64 h available, 0.375; scheduled 64 h over 1 × 192 h, 1/3. Spec 2's 8.000 h, 0.3750 and 0.3333 agree. The run's other results agree with Little's law: `exit.cycle_time.mean` 9 h, `queue_length.mean` 1 = (1/8 per hour) × 8 h, `model.wip.mean` 1.125 = (1/8) × 9.
  - **Validation.** E124 (a `pool` or `target` naming an unknown component or one of the wrong type), E125 (a second schedule or failure on one target) and the W201 skips (a schedule or failure stops the rate at the server whose capacity it changes, a schedule on a pool at every server of that pool, none of them checked) were built at M1 with fixtures; nothing changed. The adapter asserts the types E124 checks. `tests/engines/process/test_flow_rates.py` runs two models: a scheduled server whose nominal load is 2/3 passes 17 of 20 items in 1,200 s, so a propagated rate would be wrong downstream; and a pooled server on a scheduled pool feeding a server with a failure, neither checked, with no W201. Two limits are left for the owner below, not changed.
- **Reason:** spec 2 "Block reference", the council defaults "Failure", "Schedule", "Pool" and "Targets" (AKS-76, unanswered, so built to them; no benchmark contradicted one), "Statistic definitions" (the three "proposed; confirmed at M4" rows and `utilization`), "Benchmark definitions"; spec 1 "Random streams", "Validation" (E124, E125, W201) and "Tables for the `process` domain"; the DECISIONS "Engine gap" entry of M2; the AKS-107 comment requiring the shift-end test; guide rule 5. Spec 2 did not say what a pooled item waiting for the pool counts as, whether a blocked item keeps its pool unit, whether overlapping shifts add, what a shift above the nominal capacity does, when the capacity at time 0 applies, what a time series reports under a schedule, how many failure processes a server of several units has, which unit fails, what a suspended item counts as, or what availability means beyond its row; each reading above is the simplest one consistent with the rest of the spec.
- **Alternatives considered:**
  - SimPy's `PreemptiveResource` (the ticket's Agent notes) with a priority process seizing off-shift units: preemption there restarts nothing by itself, the remaining time would still be kept by hand, and a process holding units for a schedule would count as busy; counting the units in the adapter is simpler and keeps every earlier result bit-identical.
  - One failure process per unit: each unit would fail independently, which is a different model from "one unit at a time"; with capacity 1 they agree, as in the benchmark. Left for the owner.
  - A failure taking an idle unit off shift first, or counting a failed unit off shift as not reducing the next shift: contradicts "available capacity = scheduled capacity minus failed units".
  - Suspending the item that started first, or one at random: no rule in the spec; "started last" is deterministic and loses the least finished work for the items in service longest.
  - Releasing the pool unit at the end of service even when the item is refused: "Blocking" says a refused item keeps holding its capacity; releasing it would let the pool serve elsewhere while the item waits, a different policy.
  - Counting a unit that waits for the pool as busy: utilization would include time no work is done, and the M/M/1 pool case's server utilization would depend on the queue.
  - Overlapping shifts as E123, and a shift above the nominal capacity as an error: each needs a new validation rule across components; summing and capping need none. Left for the owner.
  - Utilization's time series as busy over nominal capacity under a schedule: it would read 0.5 for one busy unit of two while only one is on shift.
- **Spec sections updated:** spec 2 the warning line, "Rules that apply across blocks" (new "Pool", "Schedule" and "Failure"; Branch rules), each council default's status line, the paragraph after the defaults, "Statistic definitions" (the `utilization`, `availability`, `scheduled_fraction` and `<pool>.utilization` rows; time series), "Benchmark definitions" (the three M4 files), the acceptance table and "Changes in this version" › "Since version 0.6"; spec 1 "Random streams", "Tables for the `process` domain" (`timeseries` metrics) and "Changes in this version" › "Since version 0.6".
- **Dependencies added:** none.
- **Awaiting owner:** (1) W201 does not consider a pool's capacity: a pooled server with a nominal load below its own capacity but above its pool's is unstable without a warning (spec 1 never asked for it). (2) Under the capped reading, a scheduled or failing server whose nominal load is at least its capacity is certainly overloaded, so W201 could still check it against its nominal capacity instead of skipping it; the skip loses true warnings, though it never raises a false one. (3) Every reading marked owner to confirm above.
- **Tested:**
  - `tests/engines/process/test_resource_pool.py` (13 tests), computed by hand event by event with constant times: service beginning only when both units are held (waits, queue length mean and max counting the item waiting for the pool, server utilization 0.5 with one unit busy, pool utilization 1, cycle times); the warm-up boundary; pool units first come first served across two servers (the freed unit goes to the other server's earlier request, not to the item on the server just freed); a refused finished item keeping its pool unit (the other server waits 230 s); a schedule on a pool (utilization 300/400 and its series); the pool's time series; `waiting_items()` and `accepts()` of a pooled server; a burst of 400 into a pooled server; a line of 400 pooled servers freeing without recursion; a loop through a pooled server stopped by `should_stop`; a pool drawing nothing (results equal); a pool with no capacity (NaN).
  - `tests/engines/process/test_schedule.py` (13): the PR #1 review case, a service of 180 s crossing the shift end at 100 s, finishing at 230 s with utilization 0.5 (1.8 uncapped) and at most 1; its series (0 off shift while the unit finishes); a capacity falling from 2 to 1 while both units are busy (500/600, waits 140 and 270 s); overlapping shifts adding up and a shift of 3 on a server of 1 capped; the default 24 h period repeating, with the shift start running before a same-instant arrival; the capacity at time 0; the warm-up boundary (NaN utilization, scheduled fraction 0); 400 items starting in order at a shift start; a shift start at the end of a line of 400 blocked servers without recursion; a tiny period stopped by `should_stop`; a schedule drawing nothing.
  - `tests/engines/process/test_failure.py` (15): preempt-resume with the remaining time (two suspensions of one item, 80 s then 20 s left; availability 0.75, utilization 200/300, no waiting); the warm-up boundary; the series; an idle unit failing first, then the item that started last (shortened so that suspending the other item would change which item leaves); a blocked unit failing and still passing its item on (utilization 0.8); a unit failing off shift leaving the next shift short; an item finishing after its shift suspended and resumed off shift; an item waiting for the pool on a failed unit starting at the repair, and one whose unit is repaired before the pool gives it a unit going back to waiting; the draws of the failure's own stream in event order (availability equal to the one computed from the draws, to 1e-12); a failure leaving other streams unchanged; a failure with tiny times stopped by `should_stop`; a repair at the end of a line of 400 blocked servers without recursion.
  - `tests/engines/process/test_flow_rates.py` (2 more), `test_translate.py` (every catalogue type runs; a pool, schedule and failure translate), `test_blocking.py` (the accepts test sets `held` as well as `busy`), and `tests/benchmarks/test_benchmarks.py` `test_every_block_type_appears_in_a_benchmark_at_or_before_its_milestone` (all twelve types; the M2 test is kept).
  - Mutation checks outside the suite: not capping busy units at the available capacity, suspending the item that started first, resuming with the whole service time, releasing the pool unit at the end of service, granting a freed pool unit to the releasing server first, counting a unit waiting for the pool as busy, failing a busy unit while one is idle, skipping failures off shift, and taking the largest overlapping shift instead of the sum each make at least one test fail.
  - Benchmarks at their fixed seeds: `resource_pool` `server_a.wait.mean` 269.15 s (−0.22 SE from 270), `server_a.queue_length.mean` 2.2457 (−0.13 SE), `server_a.utilization` 0.37478 (−0.28 SE), `pool_a.utilization` 0.74955 (−0.28 SE), `server_b.wait.mean` 115.20 s (−0.47 SE from 115.71), `pool_b.utilization` 0.74982 (−0.18 SE); `failure` `server.availability` 0.79995 (−0.02 SE from 0.8); `schedule_deterministic` 28,800 s, 0.375 and 0.3333…, every replication equal. Coverage run by hand, `pytest -m benchmark_coverage -k "resource_pool or failure" -s`: `resource_pool` 20 of 20 seeds from 1009; `failure` 19 of 20 from 1010, seed 1029 failing at +3.88 SE. The other 19 z-scores have mean −0.02 and standard deviation 0.86, and the per-replication standard deviation of availability (0.021 to 0.027 across the seeds) matches the alternating-renewal value √((σ²_up μ²_down + σ²_down μ²_up)/(μ³_cycle T)) = 0.0237 for 190 h, so the engine's spread is right and seed 1029 is a reroll under spec 2's rule (19 of 20 passes).
  - The eight earlier benchmarks and the three example models give the same estimates as on the base commit (f9923ed) to every digit, and the same time-series tables (compared with a script printing every estimate and a hash of every series, before and after).
